EDIT: Found it.

Quote: TribulatioN, 2007-12-01T04:57:21Z
2) Rate Displayer
I don't really know what to call it, but maybe beneath or above the rating of a map, it shows what you have done personally to the map, lets say using letters and colors to show what you done?
Example:

R C D

This means you have rated and commented on the map, but have not yet submitted a demo.
(Obviously green = yes, red = no)

This could be a great time-saver, and a handy tool, as you might be scrolling through your faves one day and notice you faved something you liked, but forgot to rate.

But you could make that public, putting it next to a comment, therefore narrowing it down to who might have sniped it, but not revealing specifically who did it.

#418368 30 2008-03-18T14:52:30Z Pikman
2 points:

1) It's not supposed to single out snipers, but suggest who they might be. Not showing the rating gives users some privacy as to what they rated the map.

2) If one user keeps showing up as rating good maps, but the map has a low rating, you've got your man. Snipers wouldn't just snipe one map, they'd snipe a whole load of them if they weren't caught.

Example:

Map 1 and Map 2 are both very good maps. Map 1 has an average rating of 2.5 by 5 people. Map 2 has an average rating of 3 by 7 people. User 'sniperindisguise' is the only one who rated both maps. 'sniperindisguise' could be the sniper at work.

#419137 3 2008-03-20T14:41:22Z Pikman
A .txt will work, but a .doc might not because Mac users won't be able to access the file unless they have Word:Mac.

At the top of the file, put:
&userdata=

Then, do this for each level:
$level_name#author_name#description#level_data#

Level data is the raw data straight from the editor.

If you upload with Word Wrap on, you get a symbol at the end of the line that looks like an arrow coming from the text and pointing downward, standing for a new line. Getting rid of those takes a long time.

When you're ready to post the file, there's a bar here that says "File Attachments". Then hit [Browse], find the file, and click [Add Reply] to attach it to the post.
#419157 2 2008-03-20T16:10:23Z Pikman
Pressing [+] selects the launchpad for placing. To place it, you need to hold one of the following: W, A, S, D, W+A, W+D, D+A, or D+S. Each of those stands for a direction. While holding any of those keys, click in the spot where you want the launchpad, and it should appear.
#419162 4 2008-03-20T16:20:12Z Pikman
Make sure you're pressing the [+] key next to Backspace, not the one on the numpad.

Right now, it's not an enormous problem threatening to swallow us up, but it is growing. While it's currently small and doesn't need containing, we could be dealing with a movement. N isn't exactly family-friendly, either. How many parents of 5-year-olds would allow them to play games with bleeding ninjas? Profanity levels are most definitely bad, but there's about a minimum age of people playing N, and everyone seems to know it. So they take advantage of that age minimum and swear all they want, thinking it is acceptable. I have managed to actually stay away from some of the really offensive maps on NUMA, so I wouldn't be able to really gauge the extent of the damage, but the problem is there. And it will continue to be.

#423724 22 2008-03-30T23:21:04Z Pikman
Quote: Keron Cyst, March 28, 2008 10:49 pm
"The only person who this will make look bad is you."

Is this grammatically sound? It seems so strange to me. I found it at About.com.

And, which is correct? Or, which is more correct?
A) George and Lenny's rabbits are but a dream...
B) George's and Lenny's rabbits are but a dream...

That first sentence is grammatically sound, but hard to understand at first. So it's teetering on the edge of acceptability.

And A is more correct. The problem with B is that the reader may think that George is possessing Lenny is possessing the rabbits. Sorry if that sounded weird. Here is a picture version of that:

George > Lenny > Rabbits

Where the first sentence is:

George and Lenny > Rabbits
#19772 1 2008-04-01T22:27:16Z Pikman
What I mean by non-influenced voting is that when you go to a map you have not yet rated, the average rating is hidden. This could get people to make their own decision and not just vote the average rating and get it over with. It might also discourage some (not all) snipers because they don't know if the map they are rating is good or not. If this were implemented, I would recommend taking away the Top Rated link, because that encourages people to vote highly and is a target for snipers. So, what do you think? Good idea?

#424544 146 2008-04-02T21:39:09Z Pikman
I have an idea for a contest that I don't think has been done before. How does this sound?

People sign up, then they each make a map only out of objects - no tiles. They PM me their data, NOT post it on Numa or post it in a thread. Then I scramble up the maps and send each person a different map. They add tiles to that map. So it would be like an unexpected collab, because you don't know who's going to be editing your map. Two people win this contest: the person who placed the objects and the person who added the tileset for the winning map.

Judging criteria:
?/25 for object placement
?/25 for tiles (functionality & design)
Total: ?/50

Sorry for having to dredge up a topic this old, but I think a new thread would just waste space.

The man got in and saw Little Horse eating some delicious dry mango sauce zombie kebabs which were adequately satisfactory and quite filling. He was named "Ponee". Kablizzy, you need one word. Which has nothing to do with his obsession with a certain species of crocodile living in Pwn eel and USA! situated in Greater London. He said, "What has gotten into me?" Bananas. Herbs disguised as fruits. Easy to slip on. At the right moment, I positioned my foot to run onto a big pile of horse squarely up his arse. Then someone moved the pile of nasty yellow banana peels to the pit. Oh, not your PONY?! YOUR PONY IS poorly ornamented; nevertheless, yesterday my monkey fought cancer pantslessly. Or not? You I sat upon your double wookie tickle mimic; party on! Nice yo-yo, Chaz. To be, or (perhaps overly) not (yet) otherwise inclined - possibly towards preemptive organisation, nearing yogurt-like expertise. Head for the hot, stiff, erect shaft. Penis! Oh, nevermind. Yeah, Paul's oats neglected you! Pish-posh! Ovens never yell at the field mice purposefully, oh, no. Yearly, annual events transpire. Bingo! Pingo! Oingo! Ningo? Yingo! Peter Frampton. Peter Frampton! Pahahah. Oliver needed your Peter Frampton? Peter. Frampton. DOnkey NUggets ARe FOr Puddles On Nice Yachts. Perhaps our new youth prays overtime - noting Yemen People! Our notes yearn politely outwards, nosing Yiddish prosperity (or neo-Nazism). Yonder podiatry Orwellian niche yiff? Penises, orifices: not yifftastic. Pause. Okay, now yield pans, or no yolk pulls on neverending yeast. Photons of neutricity: yaptrons! Preposterous! Orthographic necrophiliacs yearn Cardamom scented wax candles, planes, oranges - no Yugoslavians will enter the large, prehistoric offerings nowadays, y'know? It's become too inundated, providing old news yet keeping it fresh. Questions need answers, damnit. How are we supposed to predict Omani necessities? Yngwie! Noun! Adjective! Verb! Interjection! The silence echoed through parabolic oscillators, nearing your pipe of normalised yardsticks. O! How the still pantaloons only need yield. Princeton ogles nesting yuccas. Please obviate narrator's youthfulness privately, obscuring nine yurts, barraging onto unruly hikers (pilgrims of New York). Passive, obnoxious, no? You pancreas owning nil-worth yak, passed our ninth yardstick before I could speculate Poe, oh, ne "Y" - Persian? Or Nigerian? Yes. Pumpkins outjump nontrivial ytterbia perilously! O, Narcotic Yeti, please outstretch nicely your pendulum, or nothing yet pawned, owing November's yawing. But nothing that God protects or names yarn priceless senior skirt sweaters. Petulance or shame, Inigo! "Prescribed," ordered Nathan. "Youngsters demolish perfectly acceptable watermelons, predominantly our nerdy youths," said the man named Paisley Owlmuffler. "Nevertheless, you shalt be thrown to cardamom undulates, never trying lemons in creative killings." "Poor Owlmufflernext year, you shall be king!" Dejected, Paisley slept within the pathetically small barn in the ghetto by the United Nations grounds. Then, after a good year repeating elementary school and beating the crap out of Chuck Norris, Chuck Norris beat the crap out of all the people who decided to think he was the real Chuck Norris and revealed himself as pottery obliterator Nyemul Yzochovich and then proceeded to masterbait on the lawn while tying shoes with his dirty hand fingers which went into his gasoline tank and back to the crazy house where he, once again, fell in a toilet. Chuck? Chuck Norris? Unbelievably, his toilet almost bursts. Soon this story will be 150 pages big, babe, 150 pages. Chuck Norris then started playing tha TETIS and another toilet exploded and he said tupid. I despise exploding toilets.

#426883 75 2008-04-10T20:56:04Z Pikman
I would have to say:

The bad:
N-arts. You can "play" very few of them.
Tilesets only containing one or two types of tiles. The same thing is usually repeated over and over in them, too.
Mine spam. Not only does it slow everything down, it creates stupid ideas.
Gold stacking. It's just too much. You usually don't need that much time to complete a level. Making it "better for highscoring" just annoys the people who have to pick it up.
Hidden objects. The player then has to replay, and if you do it a lot, the player gets bored.
Anything by Ewolfe. Not to yell at him, but his maps are particularly uninspired and he seems oblivious to what other people are saying.

The good:
Interesting tilesets. Especially the ones that have a continuous shape, but one part is open and the other tiled.
Mine pathmaking. I find this pleasing because you can't jump up, and you can't go any lower. Good for those narrow passageways.
Pretty much anything by numa_ninja. Needs no explanation. Just go see for yourself.

Quote: player_03, 2008-02-22T01:26:19Z
Keep your maps interesting by avoiding repetition. Avoiding repetition will keep your maps interesting (hopefully).
The second sentence only restates the first. You could just pick one form and use it. But that is good.

#426979 7 2008-04-11T00:57:54Z Pikman
Quote: heatwave21, April 10, 2008 08:43 pm
If all of the new authors were exposed to maps that were of good quality, they might try to imitate that good quality. It's not their fault, they just have to learn what makes a good map a good map.

Which would require partially experience, and partially playing the top rated and bitesized maps. But playing maps doesn't make you a better mapmaker.

Just look at me. I still feel like a noob. I've been registered here for a month and for two months on Numa, and yet I don't want to plagiarize a map. So I create maps different from the best. Most of the maps at the bottom of the spectrum were plagiarized. I think these authors are afraid to get their maps rated 0 and sent to the back of the line, so they try creating their own material. What I think is the problem is that these new authors aren't getting feedback on their maps, and the little that they receive, they don't take seriously. So basically, it's not due to inexperience, it's that people aren't showing them what's good in mapmaking and what's bad.

We could all help each other if we pitched in and rated and/or commented some of the newest new-user maps. But we assume that they aren't taking our advice, so we leave them no advice at all.

It's not their fault for making a bad first map or two. They're probably just getting to work with Ned or another editor and having a difficult time is all.

So I would recommend linking to a map tutorial. These are written by experts in the field who know what they're doing, and these mapmakers can learn the good qualities of maps and the things you should never do with a map.

Ok, my rant is over...

#427983 26 2008-04-13T11:00:38Z Pikman
Quote: Nexx, April 12, 2008 09:59 pm
1. 792x600 includes the border. If you just want the playable area, then it's 744x552 (each grid square is 24x24).

2. I know nothing about the program you're talking about, but it seems to me that it would depend on whether or not the program could distinguish between translucent and non-translucent pink. But maybe not. In any case, give it a try. If it doesn't work, you can always try out blendmodes (this sounds complicated, but it isn't too bad).

See, I just realized the other day that you CAN use Paint to make your images, you just have to figure out a blendmode that works for you. For example, if the colors you've putting over the foreground are all lighter than the dark gray default color, then set the pink area to black and choose the blendmode "lighten". If it's the other way around, set the pink to white and choose "darken". If it's a mix, you may have to mess around with it more (ex: hardlight), but you should be able to get it to work. I haven't actually tested this out yet, but it should be fine.
1. Thanks!

2. If the map has multiple layers, MappyWin32 will take pink (color 255,0,255) and make it transparent so you can see the layers behind. If you use black or white, the color will show up in the picture. I'm dealing with two layers, the background and the foreground, so I wonder if I make the foreground image in Paint and then slather pink over the open parts, then bring it into Mappy and export it as an image file, if it will retain transparency. The instruction manual gives me nothing on this matter, so that's why I'm asking.

And that last part...what?? Last I checked, there's no blending in MS Paint.

#19988 1 2008-04-18T21:00:19Z Pikman
Rule of Law
Follow the Laws and you won't lose.

Current task: Vote by 7/19 on the final round!

This may seem like any ordinary mapmaking contest, but it's not. Each round, the loser of the previous round posts a Law, or mapmaking rule to follow. There are two types of Laws: type Laws and number Laws. Type Laws restrict a certain kind of object or tile, such as "no left-facing thwumps" or "no 5 tiles." Number laws limit the total number of an object you can use, such as "must have exactly eight mines." Non-enemies in number Laws can only go up to 20, and enemies (mines not included) in number laws may only go up to 5. Just by following the round's Law, you get points.
This is an elimination type contest. The loser of the round will be determined by both judging and voting. This will continue until we have two mapmakers left.
In the final round, the third-place person chooses a Law, then the remaining two competitors give each other a Law to follow. The winner will once again be chosen by judging and vote.
Spoiler: Rules1. You should follow the Law, or your map will lose points.
2. You must post all maps for entry on Numa and link to them here. Do not enter this competition unless you can post maps on Numa.
3. Maps must not be NReality dependent (aka no image, DroneMod, or PlayerMod maps).
4. Submitting any profane, obscene, or abusive maps to this contest will result in automatic elimination from the contest, no exceptions.
5. You must capitalize Law whenever you post in this thread. (Nah, just kidding.)
Spoiler: Judges and judging systemJudges:
kisler
Be_Happy_:)

Judging system:
#/15: The map overall.
#/10: For following the Law(s).
Total: #/25

Judges have a two point influence on the outcome of a round, so their views can be game-breaking factors.
Spoiler: VotingTo vote, send me a Personal Message that looks like this:
+ max_ride
- bonzai
I would like to apologize to miyomiyo for stealing the Excel voting scheme, but that's probably the best thing for a contest like this.

Only contest entrants and judges can vote on maps, but eliminated entrants may still vote for mapmakers. You cannot vote for yourself, and you can only vote one + and one -.
Spoiler: Prizes1st: Three dedication maps and this:
2nd: Two dedication maps and an upcoming siggy tag.
3rd: One dedication map and an upcoming siggy tag.
Other places: A pat on the back.

Spoiler: Judging: kislerNexx - 21/25
I don't think I've seen a level where the enemies were placed as well as this one. The action was just short of being frantic and frustrating, in the part of the spectrum called "very fun and fast."
But other than these aspects, the map was so-so. You didn't try anything really adventurous with the tiles, though the scheme there worked. And the placement of the mines and gold isn't really justified; they seem to have no main purpose in the level since they're only placed sparingly. This map just barely gets higher marks simply because it's more enjoyable than 179's map, and that's an important part.

OneSevenNine - 19/25
You used the enemies well and created limited ninja space but infinite chaingun space. Kudos, this is hard to pull off successfully. It was kinda fun to play, too.
But the tileset looked more like objects jumbled together, and at points the chainguns went crazy. It's cramped for the ninja in these situations. The first part had a nice order of things, but later the map lost all of it.

#429906 5 2008-04-19T17:17:59Z Pikman
Quote: Nexx, April 18, 2008 11:11 pm
Sounds interesting, but what if the loser of a round posts a lame law? And much more importantly, what's to prevent the two finalists from giving each other terrible laws to follow?

The deal with the Laws is that you can only modify an object. Example Laws:

- Must have 6 bounceblocks on map
- Cannot use right-facing thumps

For the first one, if it's an object limit, you can only go to 10.
For the second, you can't restrict an object all together, but you can restrict forms of it.
Plus, they only have one Law to follow (two for final round, and they aren't picked by the same person), so there's no huge restrictions. Also, if the Laws conflict, then I'll just pick a new one instead of waiting for the person to respond.

#430808 11 2008-04-22T14:22:40Z Pikman
Good idea. Mastery of the hard parts would help you to complete the level. I don't see what the trouble is. Practise mode scores are already ineligible for highscores, so you can't just skip part of it, get an impossible-looking demo, and get it on the boards. With debug features in practise mode, you could just skip to the part you wanted. But one main concern. In most occasions of this, wouldn't N be falling as soon as he respawned, which would usually be the incorrect velocity and/or direction? You would need angle and velocity adjustable variables or go back to one of the easier parts and get the desired angle and velocity.

Playing the level in debug mode would be hard, I think, because wouldn't you have to wait for it to turn up on the main screen and then reset it? By implementing it into practise mode, you can get to the episode and level you want easily, rather than having to wait for it to show up out of 500 or 1000 possible levels.

You can use it if you want to. You don't have to use it. The decision is entirely in your power, and even though it would be helpful IMO, you have a choice. Practise mode is there, and highscores are there for extra help, but feel free to go it alone. Don't attack this because you "wouldn't use it." Not everyone is as good an N player as you.
#430832 2 2008-04-22T16:32:56Z Pikman
NO.

Who needs that? And it would slow down the page a lot.

#431328 2 2008-04-24T19:16:55Z Pikman
Do you mean attach the text file? If that's what you're talking about, I can help you.
First, get to the Reply screen or the Edit post screen, where you type what you want to post. Look for a bar that says "File Attachments" below the big field of text. There's a "Choose file" button that you can click, and it lets you browse your computer for the file you want. Because you're a member, the maximum size of uploaded files is 500 KB. Then, when you're all set with the post, click "Add Reply" (or "Submit Modified Post"). You cannot do this using Fast Reply, though.
Hope that helped! Also, you could put the data on pastebin.

#431674 24 2008-04-26T10:55:57Z Pikman
2nd entry:

A Pat On The Back, A Sock In The Face is a unique roundabout map where almost all the tiles are quadrilaterals. mistaken72 knew what he was doing when he decided to squish you between two zap drones, because you have to rapidly make a shift in speed. Plenty of gleaming life in the form of gold awaits you so you don't die of "natural causes." This map is amazingly fun despite the difficulty.

3rd entry:

You Got Me High may look simple at first, but it is another one of those super hard maps that pays off when you finally reach the open elevator. I don't understand, however, why xdude_ made the top part significantly more difficult than the bottom. The mines are placed strategically and have you focus more on your jumping. All in all, this map is adequate, if a bit out of order.

(Second entry is 74 words, third entry is 71 words)

#432033 28 2008-04-27T20:15:10Z Pikman
Entry #4:

Sunset Ave. by max_ride, is quite a difficult corner-jumping map, but still enjoyable. The rockets are really hard to shake off given the amount of open space, and the obvious path can be seen even by newbies (like me!). That floor guard on the mine-covered floor seems unnecessary, but still, this map deserves a look from everyone reading this review.

61 words.

Entry #5:

The thumbnail for Crackled looks much better than the fullscreen version, but this map is inventive and fun nonetheless. The tiles aren't the best, but thankfully they're easy to move around on and there's plenty of space. Gold aplenty assures you won't die from time loss, and trapdoors help you navigate this otherwise unforgiving map. Sometimes you don't know where you'll end up when you hit that switch. incluye crafted an amazing level here.

73 words.

#432867 51 2008-04-30T22:19:14Z Pikman
|||||maxspeedGround,300;groundAccel,20;maxspeedAir,300;airAccel,20
That forces jump-only maps! Sorry to resurrect such an old thread, but I just had to post that.
#432869 53 2008-04-30T22:33:48Z Pikman
Either one eventually gets you off the screen.

|||||terminal_vel,50; reduces chance of killing yourself from falling from a great height. If you jump from the top of the wall, however, you will die.

|||||jumpAmt,3; increases your vertical distance as long as you hold the jump button. In other words, you jump higher.

EDIT: More.

|||||jump_y_bias,-47;terminal_vel,100; allows you to pass downward through tiles. The movement can be stopped with an upward-facing one-way pad. However, if the one-way is only one tile below you, it won't work. terminal_vel,100; is needed because otherwise N would die on contact with the one-way.

A little pink lady lives in a little pink house. One evening, the doorbell rings. So she walks out of her little pink room down the little pink hallway down the little pink stairs to the little pink door. She answers the door. A man says, "Hello, my car broke down in front of your house. Can I stay for the night?" She says "Yes" and then leads him up the little pink stairs down the little pink hallway to a little pink room. "You can stay here for the night," she said. But she was interrupted by another ring of the doorbell. So she walks out of the little pink room down the little pink hallway down the little pink stairs to the little pink door. She answers the door. A man says, "Hello, my SUV broke down in front of your house. Can I stay for the night?" She says "Yes" and then leads him up the little pink stairs down the little pink hallway to a little pink room. "You can stay here for the night," she said. But she was interrupted by another ring of the doorbell. So she walks out of the little pink room down the little pink hallway down the little pink stairs to the little pink door. She answers the door. A man says, "Hello, my minivan broke down in front of your house. Can I stay for the night?" She says "Yes, but you're the last one" and then leads him up the little pink stairs down the little pink hallway to a little pink room. "You can stay here for the night," she said. Then she took a little pink hammer and ripped up some little pink boards and grabbed some little pink nails and nailed the little pink door shut. The next morning, everyone comes down for breakfast. The lady asks the men what they want for breakfast. The first man wanted little pink pancakes. The second man wanted little pink waffles. The third man also wanted little pink waffles. What is the moral of this story?
Spoiler: AnswerTwo out of three guys like waffles.

#20346 1 2008-05-27T01:09:31Z Pikman
It was too confusing having the other thread called 'Bitesize A Map' and having it switch over to reviews, so instead, this is the new thread. Like Bitesize A Map, just post a map that you think is worthy of being featured, yours or not. A reviewer can walk by and see your map, and choose to review it or not. Please, only one map at a time. If you have multiple maps you want considered, post one map at a time, waiting for judgment on your map before posting a new one.

A note to reviewers: Please, if you review a map, do not reveal that you reviewed the map. The Featured Map is supposed to be a surprise.

#440743 7 2008-05-31T12:18:32Z Pikman
Quote: incluye, May 30, 2008 08:19 pm
4. Tile propulsion is towerloop style, as in squeezing N between tiles to make him go really fast. Glitched tiles are a * in the leveldata in the place of a normal tile. (* may be incorrect, in that case ask someone else, because that's what I thought it was) So, for example, to use your tile, you put a full tile in the place you want it, go to your leveldata, and change the 1 into a *.

That's for making false tiles, which you can go through. For a good explanation of glitched tiles, see the DDA Manifesto.

#440765 153 2008-05-31T14:14:03Z Pikman
Quote: Arachnid, May 31, 2008 09:56 am
Quote: kisler, May 31, 2008 12:52 pm
The new review sorting system doesn't seem to be working correctly. Newer reviews are being stacked at the top of the list, and reviewers have landed multiple back-to-back features. This doesn't seem like that's the way it's supposed to work.
I should've clarified when I put it up - the top of the 'future' list is now the reviews that will appear on the site soonest. I don't see any instances of people with two adjacent reviews, though.

Since I didn't mention it explicitly in the news item: Instead of new reviews being added to the end of the queue, which results in new or infrequent reviewers waiting a long time to see their review live on the site, they're now added near the front. If a reviewer has n reviews already entered, it goes through the pending reviews (starting at the beginning of the queue), and inserts their review immediately before the n+1th review by any reviewer. So if you have no reviews currently, your review will appear immediately before the second review by any one person.

This order isn't perfectly observed currently, since a lot of the reviews were submitted before this system was put in place.

On the later pages, I do see instances of two reviews directly in a row. I have done it once, gloomp a few times, and I think miyoser too. So it's not "perfect." It might be due to the fact that their reviews were submitted before this sorting system was in place, and they just submitted a few reviews around the same time, but I think these reviews still need splitting up, so the same person doesn't feature two maps in twenty-four hours.

The reviews are also being backed up a lot. One of mrgy05's reviews was submitted early on, but is now last in line.

#441661 16 2008-06-03T22:58:39Z Pikman
Quote: geti, June 03, 2008 04:32 am
id also like author comments, and being able to make map collections. that way unreality could load a whole collection and you could play it like it was a userlevels.txt
that would be fun.
You can already make userlevel collections. Like this, which is destiny's Elemental series. All you need to do is find the group of maps you want, change "browse" in the url to "userlevels", and add on the end "&count=##" where ## is the number of maps you want. Sadly, there is a cap of 50 on that, so if you enter "&count=5000" you will still only get 50.

Example: http://nmaps.net/userlevels?q=author:kisler&count=17

#442500 32 2008-06-06T20:10:27Z Pikman
Quote: Nexx, April 13, 2008 04:06 pm
Oh, sorry, blendmode is something you can use in NReality. Basically you design your foreground and background as you like, and then you set a particular blendmode in the mapdata and see how it looks. I'll try to get an example up either today or tomorrow so you can see.
I'll explain the situation better.

1. I create my image in Paint. (I used to have black lines, but they caused trouble later, so I changed them to white.) I fill the rest of the space with pink (255,0,255) which is recognized as transparent by Mappy.



2. I open up Mappy. Since the image I created was 100x100, I enter the values 100, 100 for each tile, and 1, 1 for the number of tiles.



3. I don't want the image to separate into tiles, so I choose "Create map from big picture" under "MapTools." Not pictured: Mappy asks me if I want to remove any similar tiles. Right now, this makes no difference, but I'll probably opt out of that later.



4. This is what the imported tile looks like. The pink has changed to black.



5. And here's where my question comes in. Will any of the export methods listed in the picture retain Mappy's transparency in N? Not pictured: There is also an "Export Text" option, which doesn't act like it's being used for Game Boy Advance like the one pictured does.



Mappy page with downloads
As you can (hopefully) see from the pictures, I am using MappyWin32, and I can't tell which download that is. (Oooh, cool. Blitz Basic download! I needed that...for something else.)

So that's my conundrum.

#442866 4 2008-06-08T02:03:54Z Pikman
It's NReality only. It mostly happens when you get annoyed that a level isn't loading, storm out of user levels and into the editor. The high scores board then finally loads while you're in the editor and draws over what you're doing. This is easily solvable. Just change the mode. If you're editing your level, press [Home] and [~]. If you're playing, just press [~]. If you're on the edit menu, press [E], [T], [P], or [M]. The screen has to reload in each of these situations, so the high scores box should disappear.

#444312 9 2008-06-14T00:30:40Z Pikman
Quote: Atilla, June 13, 2008 02:40 am
Quote: Unreality, June 13, 2008 03:49 pm
Quote: Atilla, June 13, 2008 10:03 am
Did this occur with the official version of N, or with Nreality?
Scanned NReality and there is no virus found
I'm not suggesting that it's in the package you're distributing. Rather, I believe Nreality accesses the 'net in response to level data - something which N does not. It may be possible to exploit this to have Nreality download malware when loading a level.

The main reason I'm suggesting this is because I can't see how this could happen with the official version of N. It doesn't even try to access the 'net when you load level data (and I know, because it has to ask my firewall for permission when it *does* try), and as LV said, Flash does have quite a few security measures to prevent this. The only explanation I can think of is that Nreality circumvents these in some way.

That's what I thought when I read this thread. I thought maybe that the URL input section for linking to images in image maps might have been linking to virus downloads rather than the images. To prove this, I need to ask a question: Does NReality save the images to a temporary folder so you can play image maps with the image on? This would mean that the file would be rooted in your C drive somewhere, where it could easily get access to your main computer files. This is probably highly unlikely, but the 'net does it, so why not Flash?

#444991 53 2008-06-15T17:55:46Z Pikman
Quote: Nexx, June 15, 2008 03:00 am
This sounds like a great idea, but it sounds to me like the details need some more attention.

First off, a simple question: Is there a set order that each Neditor has to tackle each gym or is it freeform? I imagined the latter while reading the description, everything else seems to say otherwise. (Btw, I think freeform would be much better since this isn't as forced as Pokemon and some authors excel at certain enemies and not so much at others.)

Also, is this correct: the final four make one map once, and that serves as the final challenge?

Now onto the more complicated part.
Since the Gym Leaders only make one map and that serves as the target to beat for the entire time, the Neditors are only ostensibly competing against a map. Really, they're just trying to make a map that exceeds a certain score (that score is set by the judging of the map made by the Gym Leader, but you see what I mean). This setup seems a little too static to me, especially if one of the Gym Leaders makes a superbly awesome map.

Also, the judging is a bit strange. All judges judge the maps for Gym Leaders and Final Four and the average of those is the score to beat, but then only one (or a few) judges judge each challenging map and determine the fate of the challenger. I'm not sure if this will really be a problem, I just thought I'd bring it up.

I was trying to come up with a way to mix it up a tad, like for each challenge of a Gym Leader, one or more judges are picked and then only those judges' scores for the Gym Leader's map are averaged and become the score to beat. It would be a tad less predictable that way, but maybe you guys don't like that idea.

Furthermore, considering how much scrimmaging happens in Pokemon games, shouldn't there be some incentive for doing it? I just feel like this part should be incorporated a little more.

Okay, I'm done for the time being.
"First": It would be nice if we could choose the gym order, but it looks like the first few are predetermined. I remember in the days of Pokemon Red and Blue, you could choose which gyms to tackle as long as you could get to them.

"Also": Gym leaders and final four members make one map, which is their challenge the entire time.

I'd like to add a suggestion of my own. If you become champion, could you choose to switch positions with a gym leader or elite four member so they could participate in the competition or judge? That sounds like it might need to happen, since this competition will need an army of people to run it and even more to participate, and currently, only a few people are in any position.

EDIT: I nominate Turtle to make Turtle happy.

EDIT2: I also nominate Kimmie v .

#448206 39 2008-06-24T15:39:25Z Pikman
There was never any need for a "Delete" function in the first place. NUMA stands for N User Map Archive. Since so many maps were being deleted, those maps weren't truly archived.

Now the site is also blocked against resubmissions, unless you change the map. Resubmissions are also unnecessary. People will see your map anyway. They might not rate or comment, but if they like the map, they'll pass it on. You can get someone to look at it by using the map rating and commenting threads, but there's no need to spam them with 1748354785345 posts about the same map. Just let it go.

Most likely, we've all resubmitted a map, deleted a map, or sprayed the map rating and commenting threads with posts. It's OK, but you should make it a habit not to do so. If you're a big enough attention hog that you need to do these things, you might expect some serious sniping coming your way. If it helps, submit your map, turn off your computer, and see the feedback. You most likely will get some. Just drop this, because you're arguing for a pointless cause.

^ Second season? I'm not SkyRay . I'll be back with an all new contest I'm brainstorming.

The polls show that Nexx is the winner of this round. The score was 4 to 1.
And now the judging!

Nexx - 21/25
I don't think I've seen a level where the enemies were placed as well as this one. The action was just short of being frantic and frustrating, in the part of the spectrum called "very fun and fast."
But other than these aspects, the map was so-so. You didn't try anything really adventurous with the tiles, though the scheme there worked. And the placement of the mines and gold isn't really justified; they seem to have no main purpose in the level since they're only placed sparingly. This map just barely gets higher marks simply because it's more enjoyable than 179's map, and that's an important part.

OneSevenNine - 19/25
You used the enemies well and created limited ninja space but infinite chaingun space. Kudos, this is hard to pull off successfully. It was kinda fun to play, too.
But the tileset looked more like objects jumbled together, and at points the chainguns went crazy. It's cramped for the ninja in these situations. The first part had a nice order of things, but later the map lost all of it.

So, Nexx wins the round! I should have the sig tag up shortly, if you want it Otherwise, I'll get a professional. Everyone can have the participant tag SkyRay made. I added it to the first post. Nexx, you get to choose the Law since you are in third place.

spudz and SkyRay, get ready!

#454735 26 2008-07-24T00:27:23Z Pikman
Quote: Strife, July 23, 2008 05:34 pm
As a general note, BASIC, and all its wacky variants, are the Black Death - avoid them at all costs. Ages ago, I did a bunch of QBASIC junk as a hobby, and I still have a few nasty habits from that time.

Quote: astro, 2008-01-23T16:48:54Z
QBasic. That takes me back. Way back. To the nineties, even. Oh, to be young again. Young and stupid. If only I had a time machine, and I could go back. Then everything would be perfect. So perfect. We'd live in a nice house with three children and... uh. Basic. Yeah. It's pretty easy, but it teaches bad practices.
Quote: Sniperwhere, 2008-01-23T00:49:40Z
Whatever you choose, I suggest you steer clear of BASIC. It's a very difficult code to learn and highly picky. Not very basic at all.
Quote: squibbles, 2008-01-24T13:09:42Z
Actually, he's right now that i thin about it, unless your game is BlackJack, Slots, or Snakes and ladders, which all rely on simple random number statements, don't use basic...i remember making a game of Snake once..that sucked, by the time i was finished, i wanted to kill microsoft for Visual Basic.
What's with all this BASIC hating? It's easy to learn. Yes, actually trying to do things with it is a little more troublesome, but awesome games can still be made.
http://www.blitzbasic.com/
Check "Community Posts" and actually play some of the games.
I don't think it teaches bad habits. Blitz Basic is like a BASIC/C hybrid. The language is dumbed down, but advanced processes and ideas like types and functions are well organized and easier to use than in C. Also, you don't need to learn sentential values or signifiers, like \n for newline. The Blitz3D downloadable demo also contains a complete command reference in case you do get lost. The only reason I haven't made any games on it is because I'm lazy and I use MGB (MyGameBuilder) instead.
http://mygamebuilder.com/
Point is, if you take some time and learn the lay of the land in BASIC, you can do stuff with it.

Wow, that was my longest post in like...forever.

#456167 140 2008-07-30T15:07:27Z Pikman
Quote: The Kitchen Sink, July 30, 2008 12:16 am
NEWMA is totally ridiculous.

1. Restriction to five tags. Oftentimes I want to say more, or put variations on what I say. For example, a simple nreality map where I changed the object properties, I would put in:

playable
nreality
n-reality
objectmod
object-mod

And that would be all I could put in. What if I also had a player mod? What kind of map is this anyways? Did I use wallguards? I should be able to add more. I liked the old system.

2. Removal of the queue. What in the hell was the point of this? You get chastised for having three or more maps on the "hot maps" page at a time. Correct me if I'm wrong, but the old "map queue" completely eradicated that. I could just submit a whole archive of maps I made at once and it wouldn't matter, only two would be there at the same time. Now it's just not. I have to wait for other people and update one by one. I'm sure this isn't a problem for most people, but it is for two reasons: One, it's just nonsensical to remove it. Why? What was it hurting? Two, I have a series of "Micro" maps that are semi-popular, and they don't take long at all to make since they're so small. I could easily submit a good 10 within an hour, but I don't. I wait. And wait.

3. Whole number ratings only. I know this has been protested and consequently explained away all the time, but here's the bottom line: We want our .5s back. Look, let's compare it to a grading scheme. 0-100 percent. 100, a 5 on NEWMA, is an A+. The SECOND HIGHEST grade is a B-! This doesn't seem at all ridiculous? It's between an 80 and a 100.

Oh, and you say "It's about the whole of NUMA voting. Whole numbers are evened out by the number of people voting." Uh-huh. Then why do the ratings DISPLAY in whole numbers only? It's STILL either a D-, B-, or A+. That shows an entire lack of forethought.

Oh, and then you continue to say "It lead to ratings inflation by having the .5 on there." Put simply, NO. IT DOES NOT. Now it does! Too often to I hear "3.5 but I'll round it to 4" or something. Correct me if I'm wrong, but THAT is ratings inflation. I'd gladly take the 3.5 personally if I could just have the ability to vote more accurately. It's irritating to the highest degree.

ALL this ratings thing is doing is making us rate more inaccurately. I can't even rate "Average" anymore! Wtf!

Ugh. Moving on.

4. Forcing comments to have both fields filled in. This is less of a concern, and to a point applied to old NUMA as well, but now you can't just put in a space. People still do it, but with a less aesthetically pleasing period. There's no real reason to do this. If you're posting a demo, oftentimes you can just say "AGD" and post it and be done. But no, NEWMA refuses that. It's pointless.

5. The lack of "Top Rated" anything. Sure, it hardly ever changed, and sure, it was mostly DDAs and N-Arts. But you don't think they got 5s for a reason? Why disregard a system you put in place? Just about *any* user-generated content archive with ratings enabled has a "Top Rated." I like those DDAs and N-Arts. I would like to see them at a glance. I don't understand NEWMA's insistence on removing perfectly good functionality. If people are so concerned with it being all DDAs and N-Arts, there's two solutions to that: Stop being so wildly impressed with DDAs and N-Arts; it is, in fact, the people who complain who are those who put them there in the first place. Alternately, just add -DDA and -n-art or whatever. So simple. So useful. So missed.

6. The search function still hasn't improved. At the very least, surely you could also search for the *names* of them maps? I mean, come on. AUDIBLE SIGH.

Well anyways, that's my two dollars. Heed and peace.

Ay-MEN. How could I not agree with this?

Especially #2. When I start mapping, I want to submit gobs of maps at once. OLDMA held me to two a page and still got my maps out. NEWMA doesn't impose a restriction, so kindly I wait ten maps. At the rate of submissions today, that's about 50 minutes, and it's even more if my first map is popular.

The problem is, one of the biggest reasons behind excluding features from NEWMA is they're simply impossible to use on the Google App Engine. That's why the previous version of title searching had to go. Right now, it is still being retooled. And NEWMA hasn't given us enough to make up for what it's lost.

Riobe, I read what you said about Hot Maps, and I found a solution. http://nmaps.net/browse.rss?q=
That's the "Hot Maps" RSS feed, but it actually sorts as newest maps. Set updates to 15 minutes and you'll get information on a lot of maps in newest order. Newest has been there, it's just been hiding from us.

The 50-map-per-page limit that was imposed when NEWMA went live, I've heard, has been decreased to 20 recently. What were you thinking?! See Riobe's comment above, which is strikingly similar to what I was about to say here.

I have another problem to voice. You can no longer add tags to random searches. Yarr.

That's my three cents. I don't own seven cents.

#456647 5 2008-07-31T14:00:59Z Pikman
Quote: AMomentLikeThis, July 30, 2008 05:44 pm
Okay so I'm getting quite annoyed at people like Wumbla posting demos with framerates that don't equal the overall demo time. For instance, he records a demo in FBF and as soon as he stops having to control the ninja he stops the demo, thus giving him an apparently insanely fast completion time.

Another way I've seen people faking demos is they record their demo, then delete all of the "|0" that appears at the end of the demo. Essentially, if you then run the demo, you get a faster time. That is a cheated demo.

The idea of different reporting abuse options sounds good, but not really for this. Couldn't you just post a comment saying to ignore wumbla or whoever's demo? If you run a cheated demo like I mentioned above, you'll see it won't really work, and the number of frames will go up. That's how you tell that type of demo is a fake demo.

Language doesn't really constitute reporting abuse, though. There was a post on the front page to that effect.
Quote
  • Profanity: Profanity is impossible to police and is generally not that bad to have around. Profanity only becomes a problem when it is also insulting or hate speech--and those things are considered abusive with or without profanity.
So, language isn't always a problem, but including that as an option might give people some ideas.

Quote: Ninja_kirby, August 05, 2008 07:10 pm
BTW, what does ms/f mean?

milliseconds / frame : how many millisecs pass before the game prints the next frame. Lower is faster.

I normally get ~30 ms/f when I have nothing else running.

#460790 17 2008-08-15T16:54:14Z Pikman
Quote: southpaw, August 11, 2008 12:07 am
Here are a few topics to get you started if you can't think of any on your own but have some input on the following:
1. Spamming
2. Clustering objects
3. Checking your maps before submission (what to look/watch out for)
4. Enemies (placement, enemy choice, etc.)
5. Tile Laying
6. Using glitches, bugs and NaN's effectively
7. Nreality map tips
8. Maps in general

1. Spamming - Object overuse is usually called "spamming" when it falls into one or both of two categories:
a. Extreme difficulty. This is caused by adding too many enemies. To counteract this, play test your map. If you can't beat it once in ten tries, it's much too difficult. Reduce the number of enemies and try again.
b. Loading and playing slowly. Some maps just have so many objects, the player experiences frustration while loading or playing the map because it moves so slowly. Try to keep the play speed up to at least 30 ms/f (milliseconds / frame).
Eliminating these two factors will help you avoid spamming and allow other players to enjoy your map more.

3. Checking your maps before submission (what to look/watch out for) - NUMA won't accept your map data unless it's free from error. Make sure of the following:
- Check your object data for any "NaN" (Not a Number) data. If you put it there on purpose, leave it, but if it's accidental, delete the object from the code and replace it in Ned.
- Don't add "$Map Title#Author#Description#" before your map data or the "#" after your data. NUMA will add these automatically; all you need to provide is the tile and object data and any NReality code.
- Look for unwanted letters in your object data. These will turn into NaN values if submitted.
- When copying your data into the Map Data box on NUMA, delete any extra lines and spaces, so the cursor stays right at the end of your data. These can also potentially turn into NaN values.

I may add more to this post later.

Madman Muntz
From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia
Jump to: navigation, search
Earl William Muntz


"Madman" Muntz in one of his many TV advertisement performances
Born January 3, 1914(1914-01-03)
Elgin, Illinois,
United States
Died June 21, 1987 (aged 73)
Rancho Mirage, California,
United States
Occupation Electrical Engineer
Businessman
Entrepreneur
Television Commercial Actor
Children James Muntz
Tee Vee "Teena" Muntz
Earl William "Madman" Muntz (January 3, 1914 June 21, 1987)[1] was an American businessman and engineer who sold and promoted cars and consumer electronics in the United States from the 1930s until his death in 1987. He was a pioneer in television commercials with his oddball "Madman" persona an alter ego who generated publicity with his unusual costumes, stunts, and outrageous claims. Muntz also pioneered car stereos[1] by creating the Muntz Stereo-Pak, better known as the 4-track cartridge and based on broadcasting station tape cartridges initially invented in the mid-1950s for commercial and short music selection recording. The 4-track was a predecessor to the 8-track cartridge developed by Lear Industries.[2]

He invented the practice that came to be known as Muntzing (which involved simplifying otherwise complicated electronic devices), produced and marketed the first black-and-white television receivers to sell for less than $100, and created one of the earliest functional widescreen projection TVs.[3] He also coined the abbreviation "TV" for television.[4][5][6] A high school dropout,[4] Muntz made fortunes by selling automobiles, TV receivers, and car stereos and tapes.[7] A 1968 Los Angeles Times article noted that in one year he sold $72 million worth of cars, that five years later he sold $55 million worth of TV receivers, and that in 1967 he sold $30 million worth of car stereos and tapes.[1]

After his success as a used car salesman and with Kaiser-Frazer dealerships in Los Angeles and New York City,[1][8] Muntz founded the Muntz Car Company, which made the "Muntz Jet", a sports car with jet-like contours. The car was manufactured between 1951 and 1953, although fewer than 400 were produced.

Muntz married seven times.[9] His wives included actress Joan Barton (who appeared in Angel and the Badman with John Wayne) and Patricia Stevens of the Patricia Stevens Finishing Schools.[10] Phyllis Diller was among his many girlfriends. He was friends with celebrities such as singer Rudy Vallee, comedian Jerry Colonna, actor Bert Lahr,[7] television presenter Dick Clark, and actor Gene Autry.[9]

[edit] Early career: 1922-1953
Muntz was fascinated by electronics from an early age. He built his first radio at age 8 and built another for his parents' car at age 14.[4] During the Great Depression, at age 15, he dropped out of Elgin High School to work in his parents' hardware store in Elgin, Illinois.[4]


[edit] Car sales

An example of a matchbook ad for Muntz car lots in the 1950sIn 1934, Muntz opened his first used car lot, in Elgin, with a $500 line of credit.[7] He was only 20 years old, and his mother had to sign the car-sale papers because legally he was too young to close his own deals.[4] During a vacation in California, Muntz discovered that used cars sold there for far higher prices; so he moved to California at age 26 to open a used car lot in Glendale.[7] On a hunch, he purchased 13 brand-new right-hand-drive vehicles to resell. These vehicles had been built for customers in Asia, but could not be delivered due to World War II. One vehicle was a custom-made Lincoln built for Chiang Kai-shek.[11] Local newspapers ran stories about the unusual cars, and Muntz sold them all within two weeks, still in their original shipping crates.[11] Muntz soon opened a second lot in Los Angeles and closed his lot in Elgin.[11]

Muntz rejected the then common opinion that used car salesmen should project a staid image.[1] He realized the possibilities of generating publicity with odd stunts, and developed a "Madman" persona as a result.[11] His flamboyant billboards and oddball television and radio commercials soon made him famous. In his used auto commercials, he marketed one model as the "daily special"; Muntz claimed that if the car did not sell that day, he would smash it to pieces on camera with a sledgehammer.[12] Another infamous Muntz used-car TV pitch was "I buy 'em retail and sell 'em wholesale ... it's more fun that way!"[6] His commercials generated so much publicity that comedians such as Bob Hope, Jack Benny, and Steve Allen often tried to outdo each other during television appearances by telling "Madman" Muntz jokes.[1] University of Southern California fans would spell out Muntz's name during halftime as a prank.[11]

Muntz's car lots became tourist attractions due to the widespread publicity from his television commercial appearances. A 1946 survey by Panner Motor Tours revealed that they ranked seventh among tourist attractions in Southern California.[1] Muntz was willing to take large risks in his attempts to generate publicity. During the era of McCarthyism, he asked one of his advisers, "Do you think I'd make the front pages if I joined the Communist Party?"[13]


[edit] Muntz Jet
Main article: Muntz Car Company

A mint-condition 1953 Muntz Jet at a Monterey, California, classic car auctionIn 1948, race car designer and Kurtis-Kraft founder Frank Kurtis attempted to market a new sports car, the two-seater Kurtis Kraft Sport. Only 36 units had been sold by 1950.[14] In 1951, for just $200,000,[15] Kurtis sold the cars' manufacturing license to Muntz, who quickly rebadged them as the "Muntz Jet".[16] Initial production of the Jet took place in Glendale, where Muntz extended the two-seater Kurtis Kraft Sport's body by 13 inches (33 cm), making it a four-seater, and exchanged the Ford V8 engine for a larger Cadillac V8.[15] Later, after making just 28 Jets in California, Muntz moved production to a new factory in Evanston, Illinois, extended the body further by 3 inches (8 cm), and replaced the Cadillac V8 with a less expensive Lincoln sidevalve V8.

The Jet was featured on the cover of the September 1951 issue of Popular Science along with a Jaguar and an MG.[17] It featured its own design, with aluminum body panels and a removable fiberglass top.[16] Paint schemes were extravagant, with names like "Mars Red", "Stratosphere Blue", and "Lime Mist", and interior options included alligator or Spanish leatherette. The backseat armrests contained a full cocktail bar.[18]

The Jet was capable of a top speed of 125 miles per hour (201 km/h) and acceleration of 050 mph (080 km/h) in 6 seconds, a significant achievement for a road car at the time.[18] The fastest production car in 1953 was the Pegaso Z-102 Supercharged sports car at 155 miles per hour (249 km/h).[19] Famous Jet owners included then-CEO of CBS Frank Stanton,[20] and actors Mickey Rooney[4] and Lash La Rue.[18]

The labor and materials required to produce the Jet resulted in a higher price for the end product, and in 1954, after selling about 400 cars and losing about $1,000 on each, Muntz closed the company.[16] Today, Muntz Jets are highly prized collector cars and are recognized as predecessors to the Chevrolet Corvette and Ford Thunderbird.[18]


[edit] Muntz TV
Muntz played the madman in his unorthodox television commercials, but in fact he was a shrewd businessman and a self-taught electrical engineer. By trial and error, taking apart and studying Philco, RCA, and DuMont televisions, he figured out how to reduce the devices' electrical components to their minimum functional number.[21] This practice became known as "Muntzing".[12]

In the 1940s and 1950s, most brands of television receivers were complicated pieces of equipment, commonly containing about 30 vacuum tubes, as well as rheostats, transformers, and other heavy components. As a result, they were usually very expensive: the cheapest U.S.-manufactured receiver made before World War II used a 3-inch (8 cm) screen and cost $125, the equivalent of $1,863 in 2007; the cheapest model with a 12-inch (30 cm) screen cost $445, equivalent to $6,633 in 2007.[22] By 1954, although television had existed in various forms for more than 40 years, only 55 percent of U.S. households owned a receiver.[23] By contrast, eight years later, 90 percent of U.S. households had one.[23]


A 1951 Muntz TV model 17A3ABy 1946,[12] Muntz had developed a television chassis that produced an acceptable monochrome picture with 17 tubes. He often carried a pair of wire clippers, and when he thought that one of his employees was "over-engineering" a circuit, he would begin snipping components out until the picture or sound stopped working. At that point, he would tell the engineer "Well, I guess you have to put that last part back in" and walk away.[12]

Marketed under the name "Muntz" by his company Muntz TV, Inc.,[11] the simplified units were the first black and white TV receivers to retail in the U.S. for less than $100.[4] Muntz was also the first retailer to measure his screens from corner to corner rather than by width.[21] The receivers sold well and were reliable partly because fewer tubes created less heat. The sets worked well in metropolitan areas that were close to television transmission towers where signals were strong. They worked poorly with weaker signals, as most of the components that Muntz had removed were intended to boost performance in fringe areas. This was a calculated decision: Muntz preferred to leave the low-volume, high-performance television receiver market to firms such as RCA and Zenith Electronics, as his intended customers were primarily urban dwellers with limited funds.[12] Additionally, many urban apartment buildings had rules prohibiting external television aerials, and installation of an aerial, even if allowed, cost as much as $150. Muntz solved this problem by adding a built-in aerial to his receivers.[24] In 1952, Muntz TV Inc. grossed $49.9 million.[11]

Muntz continued with his "Madman" persona in many of his advertisements. In one TV commercial that normally aired after the Ed Sullivan Show,[12] Muntz, dressed in red long johns and a Napoleon hat, promoted his new 14-inch (36 cm) televisions by saying, "I wanna give 'em away, but Mrs. Muntz won't let me. She's crazy!"[12] Another TV commercial presented a marching-band song with lyrics about Muntz TVs and incorporated animations by Oskar Fischinger. His radio commercials, which Muntz ran up to 170 times a day, initially followed a classical music theme built around the spelling of Muntz's name.[1] However, he soon convinced radio stations to run ads more in line with his persona. In one advert, Muntz screamed "Stop staring at your radio!"[25] He followed up his radio ads with a direct mail campaign, collecting thousands of TV knobs and mailing them to prospective customers with a note saying, "Call us and we'll show up with the rest of the set!"[25] Muntz also used skywriting as a marketing tactic. However, after watching one of his ads being created, he noted that the letters began to blur and dissipate before the pilot could finish spelling out "Muntz Televisions". So Muntz came up with the abbreviation "TV".[4] He also named his daughter "Tee Vee", although she normally went by "Teena" and, later, "Tee".[9]


[edit] Audio and video: 1954-1985
With the advent of color television by the mid-1950s, the market for black-and-white receivers shrank. Muntz's creditors refused to provide further financing in 1954.[26] Muntz admitted his business lost $1,457,000 from April to August 1953,[26] and although he tried to reorganize, Muntz TV filed bankruptcy and went out of business in 1959.[27] However, Muntz's success continued in the sale of cars and general consumer electronics.


[edit] 4-track cartridge
Main article: Stereo-Pak
Attempting to combine his two main product lines, cars and stereos, Muntz invented the Muntz Stereo-Pak 4-track tape cartridge.[28] 4-track was the direct predecessor of the Stereo 8 cartridge, also known as the "8-track", later developed by American inventor Bill Lear.[1] The Stereo-Pak cartridge was based on the endless-loop Fidelipac cartridge, which was being used by radio stations, designed by inventor George Eash. Muntz chose stereo recording as a standard feature because of its wide availability.[27] Before Muntz developed the Stereo-Pak, the only in-car units capable of recorded playback were gramophone-based players, such as the Highway Hi-Fi invented by Peter Goldmark.[29] These units played traditional LP records or 45 rpm records, however they tended to skip whenever the vehicle hit a bump in the road, and attempts to alleviate this by increasing the pressure on the arm caused discs to wear out prematurely.[27]

Muntz designed a stereo tape player called the Autostereo for cars and had it inexpensively manufactured in Japan.[27] The Autostereo could play a complete album without changing tracks or turning the tape over, did not suffer from skipping or premature wear as the gramophone-based players did, and its number of knobs and controls were minimized to allow the driver to concentrate on the road.[27] The tape player gave customers greater control over their listening experiences, because the tapes never ran advertisements or public service announcements, unlike radio broadcasts.[27] Muntz sold the players and cartridges from his own stores and through franchises in Florida and Texas.[27]

Muntz audio products were so profitable by 1962 that he cancelled his agreements with tape-duplicating companies and founded his own company to manufacture prerecorded Stereo-Pak cartridges.[2][27] Most record companies did not manufacture Stereo-Pak cartridges themselves; however, the Muntz Electronics Corporation licensed music from all the major record labels and issued hundreds of different tapes in the mid to late 1960s. Muntz exhibited his Autostereo players and Stereo-Pak cartridges under the trade name Stereo-Pak at the 1967 Consumer Electronics Show.[30]


Muntz Stereo-Pak ads tended to feature attractive young models and suggestive tag lines.The Autostereo player, which retailed from $129 in 1963, was a popular aftermarket addition to cars among the Beverly Hills rich and famous.[31] Frank Sinatra used one in his Buick Riviera, Dean Martin in his Corvette, and Peter Lawford in his Ghia. James Garner, Red Skelton, and Lawrence Welk also used Autostereo players in their cars. Barry Goldwater purchased one for his son, and Jerry Lewis recorded his scripts onto Stereo-Pak cartridges to learn his lines while driving.[31]

Muntz attempted to establish a modern, trendy image for his players and cartridges. His print advertisements often showed the player installed in an appealing sports car and usually incorporated a young, attractive model with a suggestive tagline. Most of his employees in his California shops were attractive young women dressed in overbright clothing.[32]

Bill Lear distributed the Stereo-Pak in 1963, intending to install units in his Learjet aircraft. However, he soon decided to re-engineer and customize the units to suit his own wishes, the result of which became the Stereo 8 system.[27] The market for Muntz's 4-track system had faded by 1970 due to competition from Stereo 8, which reduced costs by using less magnetic tape and a less-complex cartridge mechanism. Although the 4-track system had higher fidelity since the tape speed was double the speed of the Stereo 8 system (and the 4-track had wider heads for better bandwidth), the Stereo 8 quickly became the dominant format for car stereo systems during the late 1960s. Ford Motor Company began featuring Stereo 8 players in their 1965 automobiles, and it became a standard option by 1966.[33]

In a 1979 interview in The Videophile newsletter, Muntz revealed the biggest problem for the Stereo-Pak business was returned merchandise.[34] He explained that when reproducing the work of major artists like The Beatles, the Stereo-Pak plant had to make hundreds of thousands of cartridges. But once a popular album became less popular, retailers would return the unsold cartridges, expecting credit towards new titles. Muntz was unprepared for the returns and said the huge cost of unsold merchandise eventually made his Stereo-Pak business unprofitable.[34]


[edit] Home video
In late 1970, Muntz closed his Stereo-Pak audio business after a fire severely damaged his main offices. He then entered the growing home-video market. During the mid-1970s, Muntz thought of taking a 15-inch (38 cm) Sony color cathode ray tube (CRT) television receiver, fitting it with a special lens and reflecting mirror, then projecting the magnified image onto a larger screen. He housed these primitive units in a large wooden console, making it one of the first successful widescreen projection TV receivers marketed for home use.[3][35]

The receivers were built in Muntz's headquarters in Van Nuys, California. Sony's U.S. sales division was unaware that Muntz was dealing directly with Sony's Tokyo original equipment manufacturer (OEM) department, which shipped him the TV chassis directly.[3] Thanks to Muntz's talent for mass-market advertising and self-promotion, by 1977 the projection receivers were a multi-million-dollar business.[3] Muntz was quick to feature Sony's Betamax as well as JVC's and RCA's VHS recorders in his store, setting up a showroom to demonstrate the potential for a "theater experience in the home".[3]

In 1979, Muntz decided to sell blank tapes and VCRs as loss leaders to attract customers to his showroom, where he would then try to sell them his projection TV systems. His success continued through the early 1980s[3] until he invested heavily in the Technicolor Compact Video Cassette (CVC), a 1/4 in (0.6 cm) system designed to compete with Betamax, VHS, and the Super 8 film home-movie system. The CVC format failed in the marketplace, sales quickly eroded, and Muntz's store closed soon after.[3]


[edit] Later years
Shortly before dying of lung cancer in 1987, Muntz centered his retail business on cellular phones, satellite dishes, a motorhome rental company dubbed "Muntz Motor Mansions",[36] and prefabricated aluminum houses.[9] He made headlines in February 1985 as the first retailer to offer a Hitachi cellular phone for less than $1,000, when just two years earlier most cellular phones had cost about $3,000.[37] At the time of his death, he was the leading retailer of cellular phones in Los Angeles.[9] During his final years, Muntz drove a customized Lincoln Continental with a television installed in the dashboard: Muntz claimed it helped him "drive better".[7]

After he died, his children, James and Tee, continued to operate two Muntz stores in Van Nuys and Newhall; the remainder of the stores were franchised businesses. James employed his father's advertising techniques to create splashy ads featuring prices that annoyed his competitors so much that they referred to them as "cutthroat".[38]


[edit] Legacy
The "Madman" method pioneered by Muntz was later copied by other retailers, including California car salesman Cal Worthington[39] and New York area electronics chain Crazy Eddie.[40] In Crazy Eddie TV commercials, radio personality Jerry Carroll leapt at the camera and jumped around while jabbering at high speed, always ending with the line, "Crazy Eddie: Our prices are insaaaaaane!",[41] (which was later sampled on the "Weird Al" Yankovic song "I Can't Watch This"). Thanks to his Crazy Eddie commercials, Carroll became a significant 80s icon, even appearing in the film Splash.[41]

Muntz was mentioned in novels, including children's book The Neddiad: How Neddie Took The Train, Went To Hollywood, And Saved Civilization by Daniel Manus Pinkwater,[42] The Lost Get-Back Boogie by James Lee Burke,[43] and Franklin Mason's Four Roses in Three Acts .[44]

A production called Madman Muntz: American Maverick was screened at film festivals through 2007.[9] Directed by Dan Bunker and Judy ver Mehr, it was produced by Jim Castoro, an owner of an original Muntz Jet. The film was an official 2005 selection at the San Fernando Valley International Film Festival and the Ole Muddy Film Festival.[9] The film documents Muntz's life, paying particular attention to his colorful career, and includes interviews with people who knew him and home movie footage contributed by his children.[9] In 2001, Madman Muntz was posthumously inducted into the Consumer Electronics Hall of Fame.[45]

#464971 1093 2008-08-28T01:14:15Z Pikman
Quote: Nexx, August 27, 2008 06:54 am
Hello all. Or rather, bye all.

I am here to announce that I'll be leaving for a while. Maybe a month, maybe a few months, maybe for a long time. Seeing as I am unemployed and I have no $$, I have more pressing matters to attend to, but even more than that, in the past two weeks this game has become unimportant to me. And that's a real shame. There were a ton of mapping ideas I still haven't executed, many of them involving NReality. (I really wish I could have participated in TNCC.) There were tons of maps I was planning on playing, tons of authors whose creations I wanted to explore. But I can no longer find the time or the interest to play maps, let alone manage the groups and threads I have started. I can't continue the Map-Rating Group, and the Image Map Thread seems pretty dead, I'm sure in part because I didn't update it regularly to begin with. (Though I was planning on updating it along with TNCC. That would've been awesome.) If anyone wants to pick up where I left off, feel free to.

So in summary, Real Life has forced me to take a break from this community, but I should've taken a break on my own anyway. Just to get away from it and try out other things. It's a meaningful experience.

To all those who I am leaving hanging, my apologies.

Bye for a while,
~Nexx

P.S. I would like to make special mention of notkitt, Pepi, Condog, and player_03. You guys were there when I first came to the forums, and, accurately or not, I have always thought of you guys as "my generation", so to speak. See you around, hopefully.



Oh well. At least the map's finished.

#466863 19 2008-09-03T23:16:00Z Pikman
Quote: astro, September 02, 2008 07:19 pm
Reviving old topics shouldn't be a problem in Messageboard Games.

That's the exact reason blue closed some threads. The "Isn't it ironic that..." thread had some spam, though, so that was justified.

Rules and regulations for starting a new MG thread:

- First, look among the threads that are on the first two pages to see if a similar idea has been done before and the game is still going. If it is, don't start the same thing again.
- Search the planned title of your thread. An older thread may have been done before. If search results come up, check to see if the thread is locked. It was locked for a reason, so you shouldn't try your idea.

Maybe add more?
